# The Walt Disney Company

"Disney" redirects here. For the company's co-founder, see [Walt Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney). For other uses, see [Disney (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_\(disambiguation\)).

**The Walt Disney Company** (**TWDC**), commonly known as simply **Disney**, is an American multinational [mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_media) and [entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entertainment_industry) [conglomerate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conglomerate_\(company\)) headquartered at the [Walt Disney Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Studios_\(Burbank\)) complex in [Burbank, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burbank,_California). Founded on October 16, 1923, as an [animation studio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animation_studio) by brothers [Walt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney) and [Roy Oliver Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_O._Disney) as **Disney Brothers Cartoon Studio**, Disney operated under the names **Walt Disney Studio** and **Walt Disney Productions** before adopting its current name in 1986. In 1928, Disney established itself as a leader in the [animation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animation) industry with the short film *[Steamboat Willie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamboat_Willie)*. The film used [synchronized sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronized_sound) to become the first post-produced sound cartoon, and popularized [Mickey Mouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mouse),[^cite_note-ribuneCinephoneT-5] who became Disney's mascot and corporate icon.[^cite_note-\:0-6]

After becoming a success by the early 1940s,[^cite_note-7][^cite_note-8][^cite_note-9] Disney diversified into live-action films, television, and [theme parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theme_parks) in the 1950s. However, following Walt Disney's death in 1966, the company's profits, especially in the animation sector, began to decline. In 1984, Disney's shareholders voted [Michael Eisner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Eisner) as CEO, who led a reversal of the company's decline through a combination of international theme park expansion and the highly successful [Disney Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Renaissance) period of animation from 1989 to 1999. In 2005, under the new CEO [Bob Iger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Iger), the company continued to expand into a major entertainment conglomerate with the acquisitions of [Pixar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pixar) in 2006, [Marvel Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marvel_Entertainment) in 2009, [Lucasfilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucasfilm) in 2012, and [21st Century Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/21st_Century_Fox) in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acquisition_of_21st_Century_Fox_by_Disney). In 2020, [Bob Chapek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Chapek) became the head of Disney after Iger's retirement. However, Chapek was ousted in 2022 and Iger was reinstated as CEO.[^cite_note-10] [Josh D'Amaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josh_D'Amaro) became CEO of Disney in 2026.

Disney operates the largest television and film studio in [Hollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood,_Los_Angeles).[^cite_note-11] [Walt Disney Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Studios_\(division\)) includes [Walt Disney Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Pictures), [Walt Disney Animation Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Animation_Studios), [Pixar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pixar), [Marvel Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marvel_Studios), [Lucasfilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucasfilm), [20th Century Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Century_Studios), [20th Century Animation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_Century_Animation), and [Searchlight Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Searchlight_Pictures). Disney's other main business units include divisions operating the [ABC television network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company); cable television networks such as [Disney Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Channel), [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN), [Freeform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freeform_\(TV_channel\)), [FX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FX_\(TV_channel\)), and [National Geographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Geographic_\(American_TV_channel\)); publishing, merchandising, music, and theater divisions; [direct-to-consumer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct-to-consumer) streaming services such as [Disney+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney+), [ESPN+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN+), [Hulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hulu), and [Hotstar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney+_Hotstar); and [Disney Experiences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Experiences), which includes several theme parks, resort hotels, and [cruise lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Cruise_Line) around the world.

Disney is one of the biggest and best-known companies in the world.[^cite_note-12] Often regarded as one of the most influential entertainment brands in history, Disney is credited with revolutionizing the animation industry. Disney was ranked 71st on the 2025 [*Forbes* Global 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forbes_Global_2000),[^cite_note-13] and 44th on the 2026 [*Fortune* 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_500) list of biggest companies in the United States by revenue.[^cite_note-14] Since its founding, the company has won 135 [Academy Awards](324.md), 26 of which were [awarded to Walt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Academy_Awards_for_Walt_Disney). The company has produced films which have been featured on many [lists of the greatest films of all time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_films_considered_the_best) and is a key member of the theme park industry. The company has been public since 1940 and trades on the [New York Stock Exchange](21560.md) (NYSE) and has been a component of the [Dow Jones Industrial Average](47361.md) since 1991. In August 2020, about two-thirds of the stock was owned by large financial institutions. The company celebrated its [100th anniversary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centennial) on October 16, 2023.

## History

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Walt Disney Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Walt_Disney_Company).

### 1923–1927: Founding and Winkler Pictures

Publicity photo of Walt Disney from the Boy Scouts of America. Disney was given an award by them in 1946.

Roy O. Disney (1893–1971), the American businessman, partner and co-founder of The Walt Disney Company with his brother Walt Disney. This 1965 photograph shows Roy Disney and his brother with Florida's Governor W. Haydon Burns (1912–87), announcing plans to create a Disney theme park in the state. Walt Disney World opened in 1971. Located just southwest of Orlando, Florida, the attraction grew to become the largest resort in the world, covering 47 square miles (122 square kilometers) and encompassing four theme parks, two water parks, a wilderness preserve, and numerous hotels.

Walt Disney (left) and his brother Roy O. Disney (right) co-founded the Disney Brothers Cartoon Studio in 1923, which later became the Walt Disney Company.

In 1921, American animators [Walt Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney) and [Ub Iwerks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ub_Iwerks) founded [Laugh-O-Gram Studio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laugh-O-Gram_Studio) in [Kansas City, Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City,_Missouri).[^cite_note-15] A ragtag team of animators led by Disney went on to create numerous short films at the studio. The final one, in 1923, was entitled *[Alice's Wonderland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice's_Wonderland)* and depicted child actress [Virginia Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Davis) interacting with animated characters. While Laugh-O-Gram's shorts were popular in Kansas City, the studio went bankrupt in 1923 and Disney moved to Los Angeles, to join his brother [Roy O. Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_O._Disney), who was recovering from [tuberculosis](30653.md).[^cite_note-16] Shortly after Walt's move, New York film distributor [Margaret J. Winkler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_J._Winkler) purchased *Alice's Wonderland*, which began to gain popularity. Disney signed a contract with [Winkler Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winkler_Pictures) for \$1,500 to create six series of *[Alice Comedies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_Comedies)*, with an option for two more six-episode series.[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-FOOTNOTERockefeller20163-18] Walt and Roy Disney founded **Disney Brothers Cartoon Studio** on October 16, 1923, to produce the films.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier200742-19] In January 1926, the Disneys moved into a new studio on Hyperion Street and the studio's name was changed to **Walt Disney Studio**.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler200798-20]

Black and white rabbit in pants jumping

One of Disney's first animated characters, [Oswald the Lucky Rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswald_the_Lucky_Rabbit), which Disney regained the rights to in 2006. The initial run of Oswald shorts by Disney entered the public domain in 2024.

The Walt Disney Studio at the time solely handled animation services, while all other aspects of production were handled by Winkler Pictures; Winkler eventually dropped their distribution services, as her husband [Charles Mintz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Mintz) and brother George Winkler took over as producers, finding a distributor in [Universal Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Pictures). In 1927, Mintz asked for a new series, and Disney created his first series of fully animated shorts, starring a character named [Oswald the Lucky Rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oswald_the_Lucky_Rabbit).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERockefeller20164-21] The series was produced by [Winkler Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Screen_Gems) and distributed by [Universal Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Pictures). In 1928, Disney and Mintz entered into a contract dispute, with Disney asking for a larger fee, while Mintz sought to reduce the price. Disney discovered Universal Pictures owned the [intellectual property rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intellectual_property_rights) to Oswald, and Mintz threatened to produce the shorts without him if he did not accept the reduction in payment.[^cite_note-BBCOswald-22][^cite_note-23] After Disney declined and returned to Los Angeles, Mintz hired a majority of Disney's animators into his in-house studio, most of whom were tired of Disney's ineffective leadership. A few employees, including Iwerks and [Les Clark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_Clark). Before their departure, 26 Oswald films were completed with Disney's involvement.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESusanin2011182Rockefeller20164-24][^cite_note-25]

### 1928–1934: Mickey Mouse and *Silly Symphonies*

Disney and Iwerks reincorporated the company as **Disney Cartoons**. They replaced Oswald with a mouse character originally named [Mortimer Mouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortimer_Mouse), before Disney's wife urged him to change the name to [Mickey Mouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mouse).[^cite_note-TimeMickeyHistory-26][^cite_note-27] In May 1928, Mickey Mouse debuted in test screenings of the shorts *[Plane Crazy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plane_Crazy)* and *[The Gallopin' Gaucho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Gallopin'_Gaucho)*. Later that year, the studio produced *[Steamboat Willie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamboat_Willie)*, its first sound film and third short in the Mickey Mouse series, which was made using [synchronized sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronized_sound), becoming the first post-produced sound cartoon.[^cite_note-ribuneCinephoneT-5] The sound was created using Powers' [Cinephone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinephone) system, which used [Lee de Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_de_Forest)'s [Phonofilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phonofilm) system.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFeilding1967187-28] [Pat Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pat_Powers_\(businessman\))' company distributed *Steamboat Willie*, which was an immediate hit.[^cite_note-TimeMickeyHistory-26][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDavis,_Amy20199-29][^cite_note-Distributors-30] In 1929, the company successfully re-released the two earlier films with synchronized sound.[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier200354-32]

After the release of *Steamboat Willie* at the [Colony Theater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_Theater_\(Broadway\)) in New York, Mickey Mouse became an immensely popular character.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier200354-32][^cite_note-TimeMickeyHistory-26] Disney Brothers Studio made several cartoons featuring Mickey and other characters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESusanin2011261-33] In August 1929, the company began making the *[Silly Symphony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silly_Symphony)* series with [Columbia Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbia_Pictures) as the distributor, because the Disney brothers felt they were not receiving their share of profits from Powers.[^cite_note-Distributors-30] Powers ended his contract with Iwerks, who later started his own studio.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier200775-78-34] [Carl W. Stalling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_W._Stalling) played an important role in starting the series, and composed the music for early films but left the company after Iwerks' departure.[^cite_note-SillySymphonies-35][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier200777-36] In September, theater manager Harry Woodin requested permission to start a [Mickey Mouse Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mouse_Club) at his theater the Fox Dome to boost attendance. Disney agreed, but David E. Dow started the first-such club at [Elsinore Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elsinore_Theatre) before Woodin could start his. On December 21, the first meeting at Elsinore Theatre was attended by around 1,200 children.[^cite_note-37][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrasniewicz201051-38] On July 24, 1930, Joseph Conley, president of [King Features Syndicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Features_Syndicate), wrote to the Disney studio and asked the company to produce a Mickey Mouse comic strip; production started in November and samples were sent to King Features.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaufmanGerstein201884–85-39] On December 16, 1930, the Walt Disney Studios partnership was reorganized as a corporation with the name Walt Disney Productions, Limited, which had a merchandising division named [Walt Disney Enterprises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Enterprises), and subsidiaries called Disney Film Recording Company, Limited and Liled Realty and Investment Company; the latter of which managed real estate holdings. Walt Disney and his wife held 60% (6,000 shares) of the company, and Roy Disney owned 40%.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007342-40]



Excerpt of *[Steamboat Willie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steamboat_Willie)* (1928), the first Mickey Mouse sound cartoon.

The comic strip *[Mickey Mouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mickey_Mouse_\(comic_strip\))* debuted on January 13, 1930, in *[New York Daily Mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_Daily_Mirror)* and by 1931, the strip was published in 60 newspapers in the U.S., and in 20 other countries.[^cite_note-MerchandisingHidndustan-41] After realizing releasing [merchandise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merchandising) based on the characters would generate more revenue, a man in New York offered Disney \$300 for license to put Mickey Mouse on [writing tablets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Notebook) he was manufacturing. Disney accepted and Mickey Mouse became the first licensed character.[^cite_note-42][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrasniewicz201052-43] In 1933, Disney asked [Kay Kamen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kay_Kamen), the owner of a Kansas City advertising firm, to run Disney's merchandising; Kamen agreed and transformed Disney's merchandising. Within a year, Kamen had 40 licenses for Mickey Mouse and within two years, had made \$35 million worth of sales. In 1934, Disney said he made more money from the merchandising of Mickey Mouse than from the character's films.[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrasniewicz201055-45]

The [Waterbury Clock Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waterbury_Clock_Company) created a Mickey Mouse watch, which became so popular it saved the company from bankruptcy during the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression). During a promotional event at Macy's, 11,000 Mickey Mouse watches sold in one day; and within two years, two-and-a-half million watches were sold.[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-MerchandisingHidndustan-41][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKrasniewicz201055-45] As Mickey Mouse become a heroic character rather than a mischievous one, Disney needed another character that could produce [gags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gag).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007199–201-47] Disney invited radio presenter [Clarence Nash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Nash) to the animation studio; Disney wanted to use Nash to play [Donald Duck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Duck), a talking duck that would be the studio's new gag character. Donald Duck made his first appearance in 1934 in *[The Wise Little Hen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Wise_Little_Hen).* Though he did not become popular as quickly as Mickey had, Donald Duck had a featured role in *[Donald and Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_and_Pluto)* (1936) and was given his own series.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007201,_203-48]

After a disagreement with Columbia Pictures about the *Silly Symphony* cartoons, Disney signed a distribution contract with [United Artists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Artists) from 1932 to 1937 to distribute them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier200789,_136-49] In 1932, Disney signed an exclusive contract with [Technicolor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technicolor) to produce cartoons in color until the end of 1935, beginning with the *Silly Symphony* short *[Flowers and Trees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flowers_and_Trees)* (1932).[^cite_note-50] The film was the first full-color cartoon and won the [Academy Award](324.md) for Best Cartoon.[^cite_note-ribuneCinephoneT-5] In 1933, *[The Three Little Pigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Little_Pigs_\(film\))*, another popular *Silly Symphony* short, was released and also won the Academy Award for Best Cartoon.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESusanin2011261-33][^cite_note-51] The song from the film "[Who's Afraid of the Big Bad Wolf?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who's_Afraid_of_the_Big_Bad_Wolf%3F)", which was composed by [Frank Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Churchill)—who wrote other *Silly Symphonies* songs—became popular and remained so throughout the 1930s, and became one of the best-known Disney songs.[^cite_note-SillySymphonies-35] Other *Silly Symphonies* films won the Best Cartoon award from 1931 to 1939, except for 1938, when another Disney film, *[Ferdinand the Bull](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_the_Bull_\(film\))*, won it.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESusanin2011261-33]

### 1934–1949: Golden Age of Animation, strike, and wartime era

Three story building with green stripes surrounded by some trees

The original animation building at the Walt Disney Studios in Burbank, California, which they fully moved into in 1940

In 1934, Walt Disney announced a feature-length animated film, *[Snow White and the Seven Dwarfs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snow_White_and_the_Seven_Dwarfs_\(1937_film\))*. It would be the first [cel animated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cel_animated) feature and the first animated feature produced in the United States. Its novelty made it a risky venture; Roy tried to persuade Walt not to produce it, arguing it would bankrupt the studio, and while widely anticipated by the public, it was referred to by some critics as "Disney's Folly".[^cite_note-TribuneSnowWhite-52][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESusanin2011215-53] Walt directed the animators to take a realistic approach, creating scenes as though they were live action.[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007-55] While making the film, the company created the [multiplane camera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiplane_camera), consisting of pieces of glass upon which drawings were placed at different distances to create an illusion of depth in the backgrounds.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilliamsDenneyDenney2004116-56] After United Artists attempted to attain future television rights to the Disney shorts, Walt signed a distribution contract with [RKO Radio Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RKO_Pictures) on March 2, 1936.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007136-57] Walt Disney Productions exceeded its original budget of \$150,000 for *Snow White* by ten times; its production eventually cost the company \$1.5 million.[^cite_note-TribuneSnowWhite-52]

*Snow White* took 3 years to make, premiering on December 21, 1937. It was an immediate critical and commercial success, becoming the highest-grossing film up to that point, grossing \$8 million (equivalent to \$179,166,667 in 2025 dollars); after re-releases, it grossed a total of \$1,270,588,627 in the U.S. adjusted for inflation.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007271Barrier2007131-59] Using the profits from *Snow White*, Disney financed the construction of a new 51-acre studio complex in [Burbank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burbank,_California), which the company fully moved into in 1940 and where the company is still headquartered.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007158–60-60][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007287-61] In April 1940, Disney Productions had its [initial public offering](63879.md), with the common stock remaining with Disney and his family. Disney did not want to go public, but the company needed the money.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007152-62]

Shortly before *Snow White's* release, work began on the company's next features, *[Pinocchio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinocchio_\(1940_film\))* and *[Bambi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bambi)*. *Pinocchio* was released in February 1940 while *Bambi* was postponed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007136-57] Despite *Pinocchio*'s critical acclaim (it won the Academy Awards for [Best Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Best_Song_Academy_Award) and [Best Score](83065.md) and was lauded for groundbreaking achievements in animation),[^cite_note-LaTimesDumbo-63] the film performed poorly at the box office, due to World War II affecting the international box office.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007151–152-65]

The company's third feature *[Fantasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fantasia_\(1940_film\))* (1940) introduced groundbreaking advancements in cinema technology, chiefly [Fantasound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fantasound), an early [surround sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surround_sound) system making it the first commercial film to be shown in [stereo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stereophonic_sound). However, *Fantasia* similarly performed poorly at the box office.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007309–10-66][^cite_note-67][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergan201182-68] In 1941, the company experienced a major setback when 300 of its 800 animators, led by one of the top animators [Art Babbitt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Babbitt), [went on strike for 5 weeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_animators'_strike) for unionization and higher pay. Walt Disney publicly accused the strikers of being party to a [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism) conspiracy and fired many of them, including some of the studio's best.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007366,_370-69][^cite_note-AwStrike-70] Roy unsuccessfully attempted to persuade the company's main distributors to invest in the studio, which could no longer afford to offset production costs with employee layoffs.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007171-71] The anthology film *[The Reluctant Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Reluctant_Dragon_\(1941_film\))* (1941) ran \$100,000 short of its production cost, contributing to the studio's financial woes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007161,_180-72]

Man dressed as a gaucho with someone dressed as Donald Duck

Walt (right) dressed as a gaucho next to Donald Duck in [Argentina](18951905.md) while on the company's South American goodwill trip in 1941.

While negotiations to end the strike were underway, Walt and studio animators embarked on a 12-week goodwill visit to South America, funded by the [Office of the Coordinator of Inter-American Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_the_Coordinator_of_Inter-American_Affairs).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007370–71-73] During the trip, the animators began plotting films, taking inspiration from the local environments and music.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007372-74] As a result of the strike, federal mediators compelled the studio to recognize the [Screen Cartoonist's Guild](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Screen_Cartoonist's_Guild) and several animators left, leaving it with 694 employees.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007374-75][^cite_note-AwStrike-70] To recover from financial losses, Disney rushed the studio's 4th animated feature *[Dumbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dumbo)* (1941) into production on a reduced budget; this performed well at the box office, infusing the studio with much needed cash.[^cite_note-LaTimesDumbo-63][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007380-76] After U.S. entry into World War II, many of the company's animators were drafted into the army.[^cite_note-ColliderWWII-77] 500 [United States Army](32087.md) soldiers occupied the studio for 8 months to protect a nearby [Lockheed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lockheed_Corporation) aircraft plant. While they were there, the soldiers fixed equipment in large soundstages and converted storage sheds into ammunition depots.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007381–82Barrier2007182-78] The [United States Navy](20518076.md) asked Disney to [produce propaganda films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney's_World_War_II_propaganda_production) to gain support for the war, and with the studio badly in need of profits, Disney agreed, signing a contract for 20 war-related shorts for \$90,000.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007383-79] Most of the company's employees worked on the project, which spawned films such as *[Victory Through Air Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victory_Through_Air_Power_\(film\))*, and others which included some of the company's characters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007184-80][^cite_note-ColliderWWII-77]

In August 1942, Disney released its fifth feature film, *Bambi,* after five years in development, and performed poorly at the box office.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007180-81] Later, as products of the South American trip, Disney released the features *[Saludos Amigos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saludos_Amigos)* (1942) and *[The Three Caballeros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Three_Caballeros)* (1944).[^cite_note-ColliderWWII-77][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007187-88-82] This was a new strategy of releasing [package films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthology_film), collections of short cartoons grouped to make feature films. Both performed poorly. Disney released more package films through the rest of the decade, including *[Make Mine Music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Make_Mine_Music)* (1946)*, [Fun and Fancy Free](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fun_and_Fancy_Free)* (1947), *[Melody Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melody_Time)* (1948), and *[The Adventures of Ichabod and Mr. Toad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Adventures_of_Ichabod_and_Mr._Toad)* (1949), to try to recover from its financial losses.[^cite_note-ColliderWWII-77] Disney began producing less-expensive live-action films mixed with animation, beginning with *[Song of the South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Song_of_the_South)* (1946) which would become one of Disney's most controversial films.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007432–33-83][^cite_note-WireSongSouth-84] As a result of its financial problems, Disney began re-releasing its feature films in 1944.[^cite_note-WireSongSouth-84][^cite_note-85] In 1948, it began premiering the nature documentary series, *[True-Life Adventures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/True-Life_Adventures)*, which ran until 1960, winning 8 Academy Awards.[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007208-87] In 1949, the [Walt Disney Music Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Music_Group) was founded to help with profits for merchandising.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHollisEhrbar20067-8-88]

### 1950–1967: Live-action films, television, Disneyland, and Walt Disney's death

In the 1950s, Disney returned to producing full-length animated feature films, beginning with *[Cinderella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinderella_\(1950_film\))* (1950), its first feature in eight years. A critical and commercial success, *Cinderella* saved Disney after the financial pitfalls of the wartime era; it was its most financially successful film since *Snow White*, making \$8 million in its first year. Walt began to reduce his involvement with animation, focusing his attention on the studio's increasingly diverse portfolio of projects, including live-action films (of which *[Treasure Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treasure_Island_\(1950_film\))* was the studio's first), television and [amusement parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amusement_parks).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007221-89][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECanemaker2001110-90] In 1950 the company made its first foray into television when [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBC) aired "[One Hour in Wonderland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Hour_in_Wonderland)", a promotional program for Disney's next animated film, *[Alice in Wonderland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alice_in_Wonderland_\(1951_film\))* (1951), and sponsored by [Coca-Cola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca-Cola).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007228Gabler2007503-91] *Alice* was financially unsuccessful, falling \$1 million short of the production budget.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007230Gabler2007487-92] In February 1953, Disney's next animated film *[Peter Pan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Pan_\(1953_film\))* was released to financial success;[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007491-93] it was the last Disney film distributed by RKO after Disney ended its contract and created its own distribution company [Buena Vista Distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buena_Vista_Distribution).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007262–63-94]

Several men looking at plans together

Walt (center) showing the plans of Disneyland to officials from Orange County in December 1954

According to Walt, he first had the idea of building an amusement park during a visit to [Griffith Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Griffith_Park) with his daughters. He said he watched them ride a carousel and thought there "should be ... some kind of amusement enterprise built where the parents and the children could have fun together".[^cite_note-95][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007191Gabler2007-96] Initially planning the construction of an eight-acre (3.2 ha) Mickey Mouse Park near the Burbank studio, Walt changed the planned amusement park's name to Disneylandia, then to [Disneyland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disneyland).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007488–89-97] A new company, WED Enterprises (now [Walt Disney Imagineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Imagineering)), was formed in 1952 to design and construct the park.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007236-98] Drawing inspiration from amusement parks in the U.S. and Europe, Walt approached the design of Disneyland with an emphasis on thematic storytelling and cleanliness, innovative approaches for amusement parks of the time.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007235Gabler2007495-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007240Gabler2007498–99,_524-100] The plan to build the park in Burbank was abandoned when Walt realized 8 acres would not be enough to accomplish his vision. Disney acquired 160 acres (65 ha) of orange groves in [Anaheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaheim,_California), southeast of LA in neighboring [Orange County](45330.md), at \$6,200 per acre to build the park.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007500–01-101] Construction began in July 1954. Walt formed a new company called [Retlaw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retlaw_Enterprises) to handle his personal business, primarily [Carolwood Pacific Railroad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolwood_Pacific_Railroad).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007306–07Gabler2007629-102]

To finance the construction of Disneyland, Disney sold his home at [Smoke Tree Ranch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smoke_Tree_Ranch) in [Palm Springs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palm_Springs) and the company promoted it with a [television series of the same name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney's_Disneyland) aired on [ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Broadcasting_Company).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007242–45,_248-103] The *Disneyland* television series, which would be the first in a long-running series of successful anthology television programs for the company, was a success and garnered over 50% of viewers in its time slot, along with praise from critics.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007511-104] In August, Walt formed another company [Disneyland, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disneyland,_Inc.) to finance the park, whose construction costs totaled \$17 million.[^cite_note-AbcDisneyland-105]

Children wearing white shirts with their names on them and Mickey Mouse ears

Man dressed as Davy Crockett with a rifle in his hand, alongside two men in the background

(left to right) Cast for *The Mickey Mouse Club*, which over 10 million children would watch every day, and Fess Parker as Davy Crockett in the show of the same name, which sold 10 million Crockett coonskin caps and over 10 million records of its theme song

In October, with the success of *Disneyland,* ABC allowed Disney to produce *[The Mickey Mouse Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mickey_Mouse_Club)*, a [variety show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variety_show) for children; the show included a daily Disney cartoon, a children's newsreel, and a talent show. It was presented by a host, and talented children and adults called "Mousketeers" and "Mooseketeers", respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007520–21-106] After the first season, over ten million children and five million adults watched it daily; and two million Mickey Mouse ears, which the cast wore, were sold.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007522-107] In December 1954, the five-part miniseries *[Davy Crockett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davy_Crockett_\(miniseries\)),* premiered as part of *Disneyland*, starring [Fess Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fess_Parker). According to writer [Neal Gabler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neal_Gabler), "\[It] became an overnight national sensation", selling 10 million Crockett coonskin caps.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007514-108] The show's theme song "[The Ballad of Davy Crockett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ballad_of_Davy_Crockett)" became part of American pop culture, selling 10 million records. *[Los Angeles Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Times)* called it "the greatest merchandising fad the world had ever seen".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007514–15-109][^cite_note-110] In June 1955, Disney's 15th animated film *[Lady and the Tramp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lady_and_the_Tramp)* was released and performed better at the box office than any other Disney films since *Snow White*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENewcomb200024-111]

Disneyland opened on July 17, 1955; it was a major media event, broadcast live on ABC with actors [Art Linkletter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Linkletter), [Bob Cummings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Cummings), and [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) hosting. It garnered over 90 million viewers, becoming the most-watched live broadcast to that date.[^cite_note-112] While the park's opening day was disastrous (restaurants ran out of food, the [Mark Twain Riverboat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Twain_Riverboat) began to sink, other rides malfunctioned, and the drinking fountains were not working in the 100 °F. (38 °C) heat),[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-AbcDisneyland-105] the park became a success with 161,657 visitors in its first week and 20,000 visitors a day in its first month. After its first year, 3.6 million people had visited, and after its second year, four million more guests came, making it more popular than the [Grand Canyon](46989.md) and [Yellowstone National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellowstone_National_Park). That year, the company earned a gross total of \$24.5 million compared to the \$11 million the previous year.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007537-114]

two older men looking into the camera

The Sherman Brothers in 2002. They composed many Disney songs in the 1960s.

Disney continued to delegate much of the animation work to the studio's top animators, known as the [Nine Old Men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nine_Old_Men). The company produced an average of five films per year throughout the 1950s and 60s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown2022573-115] Animated features of this period included *[Sleeping Beauty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sleeping_Beauty_\(1959_film\))* (1959), *[One Hundred and One Dalmatians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Hundred_and_One_Dalmatians)* (1961), and *[The Sword in the Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Sword_in_the_Stone_\(1963_film\))* (1963).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007585-116] *Sleeping Beauty* was a financial loss for the company, and at \$6 million, had the highest production costs up to that point.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2003559-117] *One Hundred and One Dalmatians* introduced an animation technique using the [xerography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xerography) process to electromagnetically transfer the drawings to animation [cels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cel), resulting in a transformed art style for the studio's animated films.[^cite_note-118] In 1956, the [Sherman Brothers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sherman_Brothers), [Robert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_B._Sherman) and [Richard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_M._Sherman), were asked to produce a theme song for the television series *[Zorro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zorro_\(1957_TV_series\))*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007563-119] The company hired them as exclusive staff songwriters, an arrangement that lasted 10 years. They wrote many songs for Disney's films and theme parks, and several were commercial hits.[^cite_note-120][^cite_note-121] In the late 1950s, Disney ventured into [comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comedy_film) with the live-action films *[The Shaggy Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Shaggy_Dog_\(1959_film\))* (1959), which became the highest-grossing film in the U.S. and Canada for Disney at over \$9 million,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007268-122] and [*The Absent Minded Professor*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Absent-Minded_Professor) (1961), both starring [Fred MacMurray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_MacMurray).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007585-116][^cite_note-123]

Teenage girl with blonde hair in a white dress looking into the camera

Black and white photo of a young man looking into the camera

(left to right) Hayley Mills and Kurt Russell were two of Disney's most prominent child actors in the 1960s.

Disney also made live-action films based on children's books including *[Pollyanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pollyanna_\(1960_film\))* (1960) and *[Swiss Family Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Family_Robinson_\(1960_film\))* (1960). Child actor [Hayley Mills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hayley_Mills) starred in *Pollyanna*, for which she won an [Academy Juvenile Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Juvenile_Award). Mills starred in 5 other Disney films, including a dual role as the twins in [*The Parent Trap*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Parent_Trap_\(1961_film\)) (1961).[^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125] Another child actor, [Kevin Corcoran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Corcoran), was prominent in many Disney live-action films, first appearing in a serial for *The Mickey Mouse Club,* where he would play a boy named Moochie. He worked alongside Mills in *Pollyanna*, and starred in features such as *[Old Yeller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Yeller_\(film\))* (1957), *[Toby Tyler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toby_Tyler_or_10_Weeks_with_a_Circus_\(film\))* (1960), and *Swiss Family Robinson*.[^cite_note-126] In 1964, the live action/animation [musical film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_film) *[Mary Poppins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Poppins_\(film\))* was released to major commercial success and rapturous critical acclaim, becoming the year's highest-grossing film and winning five Academy Awards, including [Best Actress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Actress) for [Julie Andrews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Julie_Andrews) as [Poppins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mary_Poppins_\(character\)) and Best Song for the Sherman Brothers, who also won Best Score for the film's "[Chim Chim Cher-ee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chim_Chim_Cher-ee)".[^cite_note-127][^cite_note-128]

Black and white photo of a man posing and looking into the camera

Black and white photo of a man looking into the camera

(left to right) Dean Jones, "the figure who most represented Walt Disney Productions in the 1960s",

[^cite_note-Dean_Jones-129]

&#x20;and Fred MacMurray, who starred in Disney comedies in the 1960s

Throughout the 1960s, [Dean Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dean_Jones_\(actor\)), whom *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* called "the figure who most represented Walt Disney Productions in the 1960s", starred in 10 Disney films, including *[That Darn Cat!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/That_Darn_Cat!)* (1965), *[The Ugly Dachshund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ugly_Dachshund)* (1966), and *[The Love Bug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Love_Bug)* (1968).[^cite_note-Dean_Jones-129][^cite_note-130] Disney's last child actor of the 1960s was [Kurt Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurt_Russell), who had signed a ten-year contract.[^cite_note-131] He featured in films such as *[The Computer Wore Tennis Shoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Computer_Wore_Tennis_Shoes)* (1969), *[The Horse in the Gray Flannel Suit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Horse_in_the_Gray_Flannel_Suit)* (1968) alongside Dean Jones, *[The Barefoot Executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Barefoot_Executive)* (1971), and *[The Strongest Man in the World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Strongest_Man_in_the_World)* (1975).[^cite_note-132]

In late 1959, Walt had an idea to build another park in [Palm Beach, Florida](109617.md), called the City of Tomorrow, a city that would be full of technological improvements.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007301-133] In 1964, the company chose land southwest of [Orlando, Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orlando,_Florida) to build the park and acquired 27,000 acres (10,927 ha). On November 15, 1965, Walt, along with Roy and Florida's governor [Haydon Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._Haydon_Burns), announced plans for a park called [Disney World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_World), which included [Magic Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_Kingdom)—‌a larger version of Disneyland‍—‌and the City of Tomorrow, at the park's center.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007302–03Gabler2007606–08-134] By 1967, the company had made expansions to Disneyland, and more rides were added in 1966 and 1967, at a cost of \$20 million.[^cite_note-135] The new rides included [Walt Disney's Enchanted Tiki Room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney's_Enchanted_Tiki_Room), which was the first attraction to use [Audio-Animatronics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Audio-Animatronics); [Walt Disney's Carousel of Progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney's_Carousel_of_Progress), which debuted at the [1964 New York World's Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_New_York_World's_Fair) before moving to Disneyland in 1967; and [Dumbo the Flying Elephant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dumbo_the_Flying_Elephant).[^cite_note-PasteThemeParks-136]

Three men at a table with microphones in front of them announcing something

Walt, [Florida Governor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Governor) [W. Haydon Burns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W._Haydon_Burns), and Roy announcing the plans for Disney World in November 1965

On November 20, 1964, Walt sold most of WED Enterprise to Walt Disney Productions for \$3.8 million after being persuaded by Roy, who thought Walt having his own company would cause legal problems. When Disney started looking for a sponsor for the Florida project, Walt renamed the City of Tomorrow as the [Experimental Prototype Community of Tomorrow (EPCOT)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EPCOT_\(concept\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarrier2007307-137] Walt, who had been a heavy smoker since World War I, fell very sick and he died on December 15, 1966, aged 65, of [lung cancer](18450.md), at St. Joseph Hospital across the street from the studio.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGabler2007626–31-139]

### 1967–1984: Roy O. Disney's leadership and death, Walt Disney World, animation industry decline, and Touchstone Pictures

In 1967, the last two films Walt had worked on were released; the animated film [*The Jungle Book*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jungle_Book_\(1967_film\)), which was Disney's most successful film for the next two decades, and the live-action musical *[The Happiest Millionaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Happiest_Millionaire)*.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECanemaker200151Griffin2000101-140][^cite_note-DisneyAnimation-141] After Walt's death, the company largely abandoned animation, but made several live-action films.[^cite_note-142][^cite_note-143] Its animation staff declined from 500 to 125 employees, with the company only hiring 21 people from 1970 to 1977.[^cite_note-AwnDarkAge-144]

Disney's first post-Walt animated film *[The Aristocats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Aristocats)* was released in 1970; according to [Dave Kehr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Kehr) of *[Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago_Tribune)*, "the absence of his \[Walt's] hand is evident".[^cite_note-145] The following year, the [anti-fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-fascism) musical *[Bedknobs and Broomsticks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bedknobs_and_Broomsticks)* was released and won the Oscar for [Best Special Visual Effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Visual_Effects).[^cite_note-146] At the time of Walt's death, Roy was ready to retire but wanted to keep Walt's legacy alive; he became the first [CEO](52234.md) and [chairman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chairman) of the company.[^cite_note-CeoPolygon-147][^cite_note-NyTimesRoyDisney-148] In May 1967, Roy had [legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reedy_Creek_Improvement_Act) passed by the [Florida Legislature](429346.md) to grant Disney World its own [quasi-government](214491.md) agency in an area called [Reedy Creek Improvement District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reedy_Creek_Improvement_District). Roy changed Disney World's name to Walt Disney World to [memorialize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial) Walt.[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150] EPCOT became less the City of Tomorrow, and was shelved and eventually transformed into another theme park.[^cite_note-151]

After 18 months of construction at a cost of around \$400 million, Walt Disney World's first park the Magic Kingdom, along with [Disney's Contemporary Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney's_Contemporary_Resort) and [Disney's Polynesian Resort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney's_Polynesian_Village_Resort),[^cite_note-152] opened on October 1, 1971, with 10,400 visitors. A parade with over 1,000 band members, 4,000 Disney entertainers, and a choir from the U.S. Army marched down Main Street. The icon of the park was the [Cinderella Castle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinderella_Castle). On Thanksgiving Day, cars traveling to the Magic Kingdom caused traffic jams along interstate roads.[^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154]

On December 21, 1971, Roy died of [cerebral hemorrhage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intracerebral_hemorrhage) at St. Joseph Hospital.[^cite_note-NyTimesRoyDisney-148] [Donn Tatum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donn_Tatum), a senior executive and former president of Disney, became the first non-Disney-family-member to become CEO and chairman. [Card Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Card_Walker), who had been with the company since 1938, became its president.[^cite_note-155][^cite_note-WalkerVariety-156] By June 30, 1973, Disney had over 23,000 employees and a gross revenue of \$257,751,000 over a nine-month period, compared to the year before when it made \$220,026,000.[^cite_note-157] In November, Disney released the animated film [*Robin Hood*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robin_Hood_\(1973_film\)) (1973), which became Disney's biggest international-grossing movie at \$18 million.[^cite_note-158] Throughout the 1970s, Disney released live-action films such as *The Computer Wore Tennis Shoes*' sequel *[Now You See Him, Now You Don't](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Now_You_See_Him,_Now_You_Don't)*;[^cite_note-159] *The Love Bug* sequels *[Herbie Rides Again](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herbie_Rides_Again)* (1974) and *Herbie Goes to Monte Carlo* (1977);[^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161] *[Escape to Witch Mountain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escape_to_Witch_Mountain_\(1975_film\))* (1975);[^cite_note-162] and *[Freaky Friday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freaky_Friday_\(1976_film\))* (1976).[^cite_note-163] In 1976, Card Walker became CEO of the company, with Tatum remaining chairman until 1980, when Walker replaced him.[^cite_note-CeoPolygon-147][^cite_note-WalkerVariety-156] In 1977, [Roy E. Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_E._Disney), Roy O. Disney's son and the only Disney working for the company, resigned as an executive because of disagreements with company decisions.[^cite_note-164]

In 1977, Disney released the successful animated film *[The Rescuers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rescuers)*, which grossed \$48 million.[^cite_note-165] The live action/animated musical *[Pete's Dragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pete's_Dragon_\(1977_film\))* was released in 1977, grossing \$16 million in the U.S. and Canada, but was a disappointment to the company.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELucas201989-166][^cite_note-167] In 1979, Disney's first [PG-rated film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_Picture_Association_film_rating_system#MPA_film_ratings) and most expensive film to that point at \$26 million *[The Black Hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Black_Hole_\(1979_film\))* was released, showing Disney could use special effects. It grossed \$35 million, a disappointment to the company, which thought it would be a hit like *[Star Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_Wars_\(film\))* (1977). *The Black Hole* was a response to other [Science fiction films](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_fiction_film) of the era.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169]

In September, 12 animators, which was over 15% of the department, resigned. Led by [Don Bluth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Bluth), they left because of a conflict with the training program and the atmosphere, and started their own company [Don Bluth Productions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sullivan_Bluth_Studios).[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] In 1981, Disney released *Dumbo* to [VHS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VHS) and *Alice in Wonderland* the following year, leading Disney to eventually release all its films on home media.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTELucas2019153-172] On July 24, [Walt Disney's World on Ice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney's_World_on_Ice), a two-year tour of ice shows featuring Disney charters, made its premiere at the [Brendan Byrne Meadowlands Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meadowlands_Arena) after Disney licensed its characters to [Feld Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feld_Entertainment).[^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174] The same month, Disney's animated film *[The Fox and the Hound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fox_and_the_Hound)* was released and became the highest-grossing animated film to that point at \$40 million.[^cite_note-175] It was the first film that did not involve Walt and the last major work done by Disney's Nine Old Men, who were replaced with younger animators.[^cite_note-AwnDarkAge-144]

A castle painted blue and pink with the bottom layer being made of stone bricks

Blue and white castle with the bottom layer being made of stone bricks

ginormous ball made of triangles

(left to right) Disneyland's Sleeping Beauty Castle, Magic Kingdom's Cinderella Castle, and Epcot's Spaceship Earth are each park's main icon.

As profits started to decline, on October 1, 1982, [Epcot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epcot), then known as EPCOT Center, opened as the second theme park in Walt Disney World, with around 10,000 people in attendance during the opening.[^cite_note-NyTimesEpcot-176][^cite_note-177] The park cost over \$900 million to construct, and consisted of the [Future World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_World_\(Epcot\)) pavilion and [World Showcase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Showcase) representing Mexico, China, Germany, Italy, America, Japan, France, the UK, and Canada; Morocco and Norway were added in 1984 and 1988, respectively.[^cite_note-NyTimesEpcot-176][^cite_note-178] The animation industry continued to decline and 69% of the company's profits were from its theme parks; in 1982, there were 12 million visitors to Walt Disney World, a figure that declined by 5% the following June.[^cite_note-NyTimesEpcot-176] On July 9, 1982, Disney released *[Tron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tron)*, one of the first films to extensively use [computer-generated imagery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer-generated_imagery) (CGI). It was a big influence on other CGI movies, though it received mixed reviews.[^cite_note-179] In 1982, the company lost \$27 million.[^cite_note-NyTimesTouchstone-180]

On April 15, 1983, Disney's first park outside the U.S., [Tokyo Disneyland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokyo_Disneyland), opened in [Urayasu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urayasu).[^cite_note-181] Costing around \$1.4 billion, construction started in 1979 when Disney and [the Oriental Land Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Oriental_Land_Company) agreed to build a park together. Within its first ten years, the park had over 140 million visitors.[^cite_note-182] After an investment of \$100 million, on April 18, Disney started a pay-to-watch cable television channel called [Disney Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Channel), a 16-hours-a-day service showing Disney films, twelve programs, and two magazines shows for adults. Although it was expected to do well, the company lost \$48 million after its first year, with around 916,000 subscribers.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184]

In 1983, Walt's son-in-law [Ron W. Miller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_W._Miller), who had been president since 1978, became its CEO, and [Raymond Watson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_Watson) became chairman.[^cite_note-CeoPolygon-147][^cite_note-185] Miller wanted the studio to produce more content for mature audiences,[^cite_note-186] and Disney founded film distribution label [Touchstone Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touchstone_Pictures) to produce films geared toward adults and teenagers in 1984.[^cite_note-NyTimesTouchstone-180] *[Splash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Splash_\(film\))* (1984) was the first film released under the label, and a much-needed success, grossing over \$6 million in its first week.[^cite_note-187] Disney's first R-rated film *[Down and Out in Beverly Hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Down_and_Out_in_Beverly_Hills)* (1986) was released and was another hit, grossing \$62 million.[^cite_note-188] The following year, Disney's first PG-13 rated film *[Adventures in Babysitting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventures_in_Babysitting)* was released.[^cite_note-189] In 1984, [Saul Steinberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saul_Steinberg_\(businessman\)) [attempted to buy out the company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_financial_battle_for_Disney?action=edit\&redlink=1), holding 11% of the stocks. He offered to buy 49% for \$1.3 billion or the entire company for \$2.75 billion. Disney, which had less than \$10 million, rejected Steinberg's offer and offered to buy all of his stock for \$326 million. Steinberg agreed, and Disney paid it all with part of a \$1.3 billion bank loan, putting the company \$866 million in debt.[^cite_note-190][^cite_note-191]

### 1984–2005: Michael Eisner's leadership, the Disney Renaissance, merger, and acquisitions

Man in a tuxedo giving a speech

Michael Eisner replaced Ron Miller as CEO.

In 1984, shareholders Roy E. Disney, [Sid Bass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sid_Bass), Lillian and [Diane Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diane_Disney_Miller), and [Irwin L. Jacobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irwin_L._Jacobs)—who together [owned about 36% of the shares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_financial_battle_for_Disney?action=edit\&redlink=1), forced out CEO Miller and replaced him with [Michael Eisner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Eisner), a former president of [Paramount Pictures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramount_Pictures), and appointed [Frank Wells](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_Wells) as president.[^cite_note-192] Eisner's first act was to make it a major film studio, which at the time it was not considered. Eisner appointed [Jeffrey Katzenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeffrey_Katzenberg) as chairman and Roy E. Disney as head of animation. Eisner wanted to produce an animated film every 18 months rather than four years, as the company had been doing. To help with the film division, the company started making [Saturday-morning cartoons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturday-morning_cartoon) to create new Disney characters for merchandising and produced films through Touchstone. Under Eisner, Disney became more involved with television, creating Touchstone Television and producing the television sitcom *[The Golden Girls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Golden_Girls)*, which was a hit. The company spent \$15 million promoting its theme parks, raising visitor numbers by 10%.[^cite_note-193][^cite_note-194] In 1984, Disney produced *[The Black Cauldron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Black_Cauldron_\(film\))*, then the most-expensive animated movie at \$40 million, its first animated film to feature computer-generated imagery, and its first PG-rated animation because of its adult themes. The film was a box-office failure, leading the company to move the animation department from the studio in Burbank to a warehouse in [Glendale, California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glendale,_California).[^cite_note-195] The film-financing partnership [Silver Screen Partners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver_Screen_Partners) II, which was organized in 1985, financed films for Disney with \$193 million. In January 1987, Silver Screen Partners III began financing movies for Disney with \$300 million raised by [E.F. Hutton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E.F._Hutton), the largest amount raised for a film-financing limited partnership.[^cite_note-196] Silver Screen IV was also set up to finance Disney's studios.[^cite_note-LaTimesSilverScreen-197]

In 1986, the company changed its name from Walt Disney Productions to the Walt Disney Company, stating the old name only referred to the film industry.[^cite_note-198] With Disney's animation industry declining, the animation department needed its next movie *[The Great Mouse Detective](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Great_Mouse_Detective)* to be a success. It grossed \$25 million at the box office, becoming a much-needed financial success.[^cite_note-199] To generate more revenue from merchandising, the company opened its first retail store [Disney Store](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Store) in Glendale in 1987. Because of its success, the company opened two more in California, and by 1990, it had 215 throughout the United States.[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201] In 1989, the company garnered \$411 million in revenue and made a profit of \$187 million.[^cite_note-202] In 1987, the company signed an agreement with the [Government of France](3398091.md) to build a resort named [Euro Disneyland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disneyland_Paris) in Paris; it would consist of two theme parks named [Disneyland Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disneyland_Park_\(Paris\)) and [Walt Disney Studios Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walt_Disney_Studios_Park), a golf course, and 6 hotels.[^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204]



Hollywood Studios' Park icon, the Chinese Theatre

In 1988, Disney's 27th animated film *[Oliver & Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_&_Company)* was released the same day as that of former Disney animator Don Bluth's *[The Land Before Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Land_Before_Time_\(film\))*. *Oliver & Company* out-competed *The Land Before Time*, becoming the first animated film to gross over \$100 million in its initial release, and the highest-grossing animated film in its initial run.[^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206] Disney became the box-office-leading Hollywood studio for the first time, with films such as *[Who Framed Roger Rabbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Who_Framed_Roger_Rabbit)* (1988), *[Three Men and a Baby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Men_and_a_Baby)* (1987)*,* and *[Good Morning, Vietnam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_Morning,_Vietnam)* (1987). The company's gross revenue went from \$165 million in 1983 to \$876 million in 1987, and operating income went from −\$33 million in 1983 to +130 million in 1987. The studio's net income rose by 66%, along with a 26% growth in revenue. *Los Angeles Times* called Disney's recovery "a real rarity in the corporate world".[^cite_note-207] On May 1, 1989, Disney opened Disney-MGM Studios, its third amusement park at Walt Disney World, and later became [Hollywood Studios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney's_Hollywood_Studios). The new park demonstrated to visitors the movie-making process, until 2008, when it was changed to make guests feel they are in movies.[^cite_note-208] Following the opening of Disney-MGM Studios, Disney opened the water park [Typhoon Lagoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney's_Typhoon_Lagoon) in June 1989; in 2022 it had 1.9 million visitors and was the most popular water park in the world.[^cite_note-209][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEButlerRussell2010160-210] Also in 1989, Disney signed an agreement-in-principle to acquire [The Jim Henson Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jim_Henson_Company) from its founder. The deal included Henson's programming library and [Muppet characters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Muppets)—excluding the Muppets created for *[Sesame Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesame_Street)*—as well as Henson's personal creative services. Henson, however, died in May 1990 before the deal was completed, resulting in the companies terminating [merger](20769.md) negotiations.[^cite_note-211][^cite_note-212][^cite_note-213]

On November 17, 1989, Disney released *[The Little Mermaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Little_Mermaid_\(1989_film\))*, which was the start of the [Disney Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Renaissance), a period in which the company released hugely successful and critically acclaimed animated films. *The Little Mermaid* became the animated film with the highest gross from its initial run and garnered \$233 million at the box office; it won two Academy Awards; Best Original Score and Best Original Song for "[Under the Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Under_the_Sea)".[^cite_note-214][^cite_note-TimeRenaissance-215] During the Disney Renaissance, composer [Alan Menken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Menken) and lyricist [Howard Ashman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Ashman) wrote several Disney songs until Ashman died in 1991. Together they wrote 6 songs nominated for Academy Awards; with two winning songs—"Under the Sea" and "[Beauty and the Beast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beauty_and_the_Beast_\(Disney_song\))".[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217] To produce music geared for the mainstream, including music for movie soundtracks, Disney founded the recording label [Hollywood Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hollywood_Records) on January 1, 1990.[^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219] In September 1990, Disney arranged for financing of up to \$200 million by a unit of [Nomura Securities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomura_Securities) for [Interscope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interscope_Communications) films made for Disney. On October 23, Disney formed [Touchwood Pacific Partners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Touchwood_Pacific_Partners), which replaced the Silver Screen Partnership series as the company's movie studios' primary source of funding.[^cite_note-LaTimesSilverScreen-197] Disney's first animated sequel *[The Rescuers Down Under](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Rescuers_Down_Under)* was released on November 16, 1990, and created using [Computer Animation Production System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_Animation_Production_System) (CAPS), digital software developed by Disney and [Pixar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pixar)—the computer division of [Lucasfilm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucasfilm)—becoming the first feature film to be entirely created digitally.[^cite_note-TimeRenaissance-215][^cite_note-DisneyInventions-220] Although the film struggled in the box office, grossing \$47 million, it received positive reviews.[^cite_note-221][^cite_note-222] In 1991, Disney and Pixar agreed to a deal to make three films together, the first one being *[Toy Story](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toy_Story)*.[^cite_note-223]

[Dow Jones & Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dow_Jones_&_Company), wanting to replace 3 companies in its [industrial average](47361.md), chose to add Disney in May 1991, stating Disney "reflects the importance of entertainment and leisure activities in the economy".[^cite_note-224] Disney's next animated film *[Beauty and the Beast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beauty_and_the_Beast_\(1991_film\))* was released on November 13, 1991, and grossed nearly \$346 million during its initial release.[^cite_note-225][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldberg2016173-226] It was the first animated film to win a [Golden Globe](142724.md) for [Best Picture](186059.md), and it received 6 Academy Award nominations, becoming the first animation nominated for Best Picture; it won Best Score, [Best Sound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Award_for_Best_Sound), and Best Song.[^cite_note-227] The film was critically acclaimed, with some critics considering it to be the best Disney film.[^cite_note-228][^cite_note-229] To coincide with the 1992 release of *[The Mighty Ducks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mighty_Ducks_\(film\))*, Disney founded the [National Hockey League](21809.md) team [The Mighty Ducks of Anaheim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Mighty_Ducks_of_Anaheim).[^cite_note-230] Disney's next animated feature *[Aladdin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aladdin_\(1992_Disney_film\))* was released on November 11, 1992, and grossed \$504 million, becoming the highest-grossing animated film to that point, and the first animated film to gross a half-billion dollars.[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-232] It won two Academy Awards—Best Song for "[A Whole New World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Whole_New_World)" and Best Score;[^cite_note-233] and "A Whole New World" was the first and so far the only Disney song to win the [Grammy](44635.md) for [Song of the Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammy_Award_for_Song_of_the_Year).[^cite_note-234][^cite_note-235] For \$60 million, Disney broadened its range of mature-audience films by acquiring independent film distributor [Miramax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miramax) Films in 1993.[^cite_note-236][^cite_note-237] The same year, in a venture with [The Nature Conservancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Nature_Conservancy), Disney purchased 8,500 acres (3,439 ha) of [Everglades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Everglades) headwaters in Florida to protect native animals and plant species, establishing the [Disney Wilderness Preserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Wilderness_Preserve).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERutherford201178-238]

Man in suit and glasses

Jeffrey Katzenberg was chairman of the Walt Disney Studios from 1984 to 1995.

On April 3, 1994, Frank Wells died in a helicopter crash; he, Eisner, and Katzenberg helped the company's market value go from \$2 billion to \$22 billion since taking office in 1984.[^cite_note-239] On June 15 the same year, *[The Lion King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lion_King)* was released and was a massive success, becoming the second-highest-grossing film of all time behind *[Jurassic Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurassic_Park_\(film\))* and the highest-grossing animated film of all time, with a gross total of \$763 million during its initial release.[^cite_note-240][^cite_note-241][^cite_note-242] It was critically praised and garnered two Academy Awards—Best Score and Best Song for "[Can You Feel the Love Tonight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Can_You_Feel_the_Love_Tonight)".[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] Soon after its release, Katzenberg left the company after Eisner refused to promote him to president. After leaving, he co-founded film studio [DreamWorks SKG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DreamWorks_Pictures).[^cite_note-245] Wells was later replaced with one of Eisner's friends [Michael Ovitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Ovitz) on August 13, 1995.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247] In 1994, Disney wanted to buy one of the major U.S. television networks ABC, NBC, or CBS, which would give the company guaranteed distribution for its programming. Eisner planned to buy NBC, but the deal was canceled because [General Electric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Electric) wanted to keep a majority stake.[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249] In 1994, Disney's annual revenue reached \$10 billion, 48% coming from film, 34% from theme parks, and 18% from merchandising. Disney's total net income was up 25% from the previous year at \$1.1 billion.[^cite_note-SunDisneyAbc-250] Grossing over \$346 million, *[Pocahontas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pocahontas_\(1995_film\))* was released on June 16, garnering the Academy Awards for Best Musical or Comedy Score and Best Song for "[Colors of the Wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colors_of_the_Wind)".[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-252] Pixar's and Disney's first co-release was the first-ever fully computer-generated film *Toy Story*, which was released on November 19, 1995, to critical acclaim and an end-run gross total of \$361 million. The film won the [Special Achievement Academy Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Achievement_Academy_Award) and was the first animated film to be nominated for [Best Original Screenplay](47125790.md).[^cite_note-253][^cite_note-254]

In 1995, Disney announced the \$19 billion acquisition of television network [Capital Cities/ABC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_Cities/ABC) Inc., which was then the 2nd-largest corporate takeover in U.S. history. Through the deal, Disney would obtain broadcast network ABC, an 80% majority stake in sports networks [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN) and [ESPN 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN2), 50% in [Lifetime Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lifetime_\(TV_network\)), a majority stake of [DIC Entertainment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DIC_Entertainment), and a 38% minority stake in [A\&E Television Networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A+E_Networks).[^cite_note-SunDisneyAbc-250][^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256] Following the deal, the company started [Radio Disney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_Disney), a youth-focused radio program on [ABC Radio Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_Radio_Network), on November 18, 1996.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGoldberg2016197-257][^cite_note-258] The Walt Disney Company launched its official website [disney.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney.com) on February 22, 1996, mainly to promote its theme parks and merchandise.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEMadejNewton2020147-259] On June 19, the company's next animated film *[The Hunchback of Notre Dame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hunchback_of_Notre_Dame_\(1996_film\))* was released, grossing \$325 million at the box office.[^cite_note-260] Because Ovitz's management style was different from Eisner's, Ovitz was fired as the company's president in 1996.[^cite_note-261] Disney lost a \$10.4 million lawsuit in September 1997 to Marsu B.V. over Disney's failure to produce as contracted 13 half-hours *[Marsupilami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsupilami)* cartoon shows. Instead, Disney felt other internal "hot properties" deserved the company's attention.[^cite_note-262] Disney, which since 1996 had owned a 25% stake in the [Major League Baseball](38776.md) team [California Angels](1360083.md), bought out the team in 1998 for \$110 million, renamed it Anaheim Angels and renovated the stadium for \$100 million.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264] *[Hercules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hercules_\(1997_film\))* (1997) was released on June 13, and underperformed compared to earlier films, grossing \$252 million.[^cite_note-265] On February 24, Disney and Pixar signed a ten-year contract to make five films, with Disney as distributor. They would share the cost, profits, and logo credits, calling the films Disney-Pixar productions.[^cite_note-266] During the Disney Renaissance, film division Touchstone also saw success with film such as *[Pretty Woman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pretty_Woman)* (1990), which has the highest number of ticket sales in the U.S. for a [romantic comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romantic_comedy) and grossed \$463 million;[^cite_note-267][^cite_note-268] *[Sister Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_Act)* (1992), which was one of the financially successful comedies of the early 1990s, grossing \$231 million;[^cite_note-269] action film *[Con Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Con_Air)* (1997), which grossed \$224 million;[^cite_note-270] and the highest-grossing film of 1998 at \$553 million *[Armageddon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armageddon_\(1998_film\))*.[^cite_note-1998BoxOffice-271]

Ginormous tree along with other shrubbery

Mainly white cruise ship out in the ocean

(left to right) Disney's Animal Kingdom's, the largest theme park, main icon the Tree of Life, and Disney Cruise Line's first cruise ship *Disney Magic*, which first set sail on July 30, 1998

At Disney World, the company opened [Disney's Animal Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney's_Animal_Kingdom), the largest theme park in the world covering 580 acres (230 ha) on [Earth Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_Day), April 22, 1998. It had six animal-themed lands, over 2,000 animals, and the [Tree of Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tree_of_Life_\(Disney\)) at its center.[^cite_note-272][^cite_note-273] Receiving positive reviews, Disney's next animated films *[Mulan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulan_\(1998_film\))* and Disney-Pixar film *[A Bug's Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_Bug's_Life)* were released on June 5 and November 20, 1998.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] Mulan became the year's sixth-highest-grossing film at \$304 million, and *A Bug's Life* was the year's fifth-highest at \$363 million.[^cite_note-1998BoxOffice-271] In a \$770-million transaction, on June 18, Disney bought a 43% stake of Internet search engine [Infoseek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infoseek) for \$70 million, also giving it Infoseek-acquired [Starwave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starwave).[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-277] Starting web portal [Go.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Go.com) in a joint venture with Infoseek in January 1999, Disney acquired the rest of Infoseek later that year.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279] After unsuccessful negotiations with cruise lines [Carnival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnival_Cruise_Line) and [Royal Caribbean International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Caribbean_International), in 1994, Disney announced it would start its own cruise-line operation in 1998.[^cite_note-280][^cite_note-281] The first two ships of the [Disney Cruise Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Cruise_Line) were named *[Disney Magic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Magic)* and *[Disney Wonder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disney_Wonder)*, and built by [Fincantieri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fincantieri) in Italy. To accompany the cruises, Disney bought [Gorda Cay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gorda_Cay) as the line's private island, and spent \$25 million remodeling it and renaming it Castaway Cay. On July 30, 1998, *Disney Magic* set sail as the line's first voyage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESaunders201376–78-282]

man in a chair

Roy E. Disney, Roy O. Disney's son, was head of the animation department until 2003.

Marking the end of the Disney Renaissance, [*Tarzan*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarzan_\(1999_film\)) (1999) was released on June 12, garnering \$448 million at the box office and critical acclaim; it claimed the Academy Award for Best Original Song for [Phil Collins](153557.md)' "[You'll Be in My Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/You'll_Be_in_My_Heart)".[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] Disney-Pixar film *[Toy Story 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toy_Story_2)* was released on November 13, garnering praise and \$511 million at the box office.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] To replace Ovitz, Eisner named ABC network chief [Bob Iger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Iger) Disney's president and [chief operating officer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_operating_officer) in January 2000.[^cite_note-289][^cite_note-290] In November, Disney sold DIC Entertainment back to [Andy Heyward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Heyward).[^cite_note-291] Disney had another huge success with Pixar when they released *[Monsters, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsters,_Inc.)* in 2001. Later, Disney bought children's cable network [Fox Family Worldwide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Family_Worldwide) for \$3 billion and the assumption of \$2.3 billion in debt.
